Positivity of automorphic vector bundles on unitary Shimura varieties
Deding Yang

Abstract




Abstract

Let
X
be the special fiber of a unitary Shimura variety of hyperspecial level at a prime
p
inert in the totally real field
F
. Let
Y\to X
be the associated flag space. For every
L
-dominant weight
\lambda
, let
\mathcal{L}_{Y}(\lambda)
denote the corresponding automorphic line bundle. We give an explicit necessary and sufficient criterion, in terms of the signature data and the coordinates of
\lambda
, for the ampleness of
\mathcal{L}_{Y}(\lambda)
. The criterion generalizes the known ample cone for Hilbert modular and
U(2)
-Shimura varieties. The proof develops the machinery of the description of certain Ekedahl–Oort strata, a geometric Jacquet–Langlands correspondence between strata of unitary Shimura varieties with different signatures, and the construction of stratum Hasse invariants, and introduced a way to systematically deal with combinatorical data in the higher rank case.

Contents

1  Introduction

1.1 Overview

Shimura varieties are higher dimensional generalizations of modular curves. The study of the cohomology of certain local systems or vector bundles on the Shimura variety relates Galois representations and automorphic forms, known as a typical approach towards the Langlands program. The past few decades have witnessed substantial developments in this theory (See, for example,
[
11
,
22
]
for some modern survey). The main focus of this paper is on the coherent cohomology of automorphic vector bundles. Knowledge of vanishing theorems of coherent cohomology is an important input for modularity lifting theorems (see
[
21
,
3
]
for the Taylor–Wiles method case; see also
[
8
]
for beyond the Taylor–Wiles case; see also
[
32
,
31
]
for their vanishing theorems). Therefore, we believe that it is natural to ask for an ampleness criterion for automorphic vector bundles. By Kodaira type vanishing results, this directly implies vanishing theorems of coherent cohomology.

On the other hand, the study of positivity for algebraic varieties is itself of great importance, especially in the characteristic-
p
case. The existence of Frobenius structures leads to the emergence of many interesting —- and sometimes peculiar —- subvarieties. In the context of PEL-type Shimura varieties, one could use the moduli interpretation to obtain a very concrete description of these subvarieties. The study of these subvarieties leads us to establish a geometric Jacquet–Langlands correspondence in characteristic
p
. This correspondence helps us understand the intersection numbers over the special fiber, which finally result in a
complete
answer of the ampleness criterion of automorphic line bundles.

In the following, we first explain our main result. After that, we explain the strategy of our proof and the relations to arithmetic applications.

Let
(G,X)
be a Shimura datum. Upon fixing a neat compact open subgroup
K\subseteq G(\mdmathbb{A}_{f})
, we obtain an algebraic variety
Sh_{K}(G,X)
that is defined over the reflex field
E=E(G,X)
:

Sh_{K}(G,X)(\mdmathbb{C})=G(\mdmathbb{Q})\backslash X\times(G(\mdmathbb{A}_{f})/K).

(1)

We further assume that
G_{\mdmathbb{Q}_{p}}
is unramified, i.e., it is quasi-split and split over an unramified extension of
\mdmathbb{Q}_{p}
. We assume that
K\subseteq G(\mdmathbb{A}_{f})
is of the form
K^{p}K_{p}
, where
K^{p}\subseteq G(\mdmathbb{A}_{f}^{(p)})
and
K_{p}\subseteq G(\mdmathbb{Q}_{p})
is a hyperspecial subgroup. We have the following results of
[
27
,
45
]
:

Theorem 1.1
.

If
(G,X)
is of abelian type, then
Sh_{K}(G,X)
admits a smooth canonical integral model
\mathscr{S}_{K}(G,X)
over
\mathcal{O}_{E,(p)}
.

Let
S_{K}(G)=\mathscr{S}_{K}(G,X)_{k}
be the geometric special fiber of the integral model. Here,
k=\overline{\mdmathbb{F}}_{p}
and we omit
X
from the notation for simplicity. For any
L
-dominant weight
\lambda
, where
L
is the Levi subgroup of the parabolic subgroup of
G
that corresponds to the Hodge filtration, there is an
automorphic vector bundle

\mathcal{V}(\lambda)
on
Sh_{K}(G,X)
. The automorphic vector bundle
\mathcal{V}(\lambda)
extends to
\mathscr{S}_{K}(G)
, and we still denote its pullback to the special fiber by
\mathcal{V}(\lambda)
. The purpose of this paper is to study the positivity of
\mathcal{V}(\lambda)
. To illustrate this, we first consider an example.

Example:
Let
F/\mdmathbb{Q}
be a totally real field in which
p
is inert. Let
\{\tau_{1},\dots,\tau_{f}\}
be the set of all real embeddings of
F
. If we fix an isomorphism
\bar{\mdmathbb{Q}}_{p}\simeq\mdmathbb{C}
, we can view the
\tau_{i}
’s as
p
-adic embeddings, and may order them such that
\sigma^{-1}\tau_{i}=\tau_{i+1}
. Here we view the subscripts as modulo
f
, and
\sigma
is the Frobenius endomorphism. Let
G=\textnormal{Res}_{F/\mdmathbb{Q}}GL_{2,F}
and
K_{p}=GL_{2}(\mathcal{O}_{F})
. For a sufficiently small tame level
K^{p}\subseteq G(\mdmathbb{A}_{f}^{(p)})
, the corresponding algebraic variety
Sh_{K}(G)
is known as the Hilbert modular variety. Its integral model
\mathscr{S}_{K}(G)/\mathcal{O}_{F}
is the moduli space of abelian schemes with a certain polarization, an action of
\mathcal{O}_{F}
, and a level structure. (This is not entirely accurate. One needs to twist the moduli problem. See
[
44
]
for a detailed explanation.)

The universal abelian scheme carries an
\mathcal{O}_{F}
-action, and thus the Hodge filtration

0\longrightarrow\omega_{\mathcal{A}^{\vee}/S_{K}(G)}\longrightarrow H_{1}^{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{A}/S_{K}(G))\longrightarrow\textnormal{Lie}_{\mathcal{A}/S_{K}(G)}\longrightarrow 0

(2)

decomposes into
\tau_{i}
-components for
1\leq i\leq f
. We denote by
\omega_{i}
the subsheaf of
\omega_{\mathcal{A}^{\vee}/S_{K}(G)}
on which
\mathcal{O}_{F}
acts via
\tau_{i}
. In fact,
\omega_{i}
is a line bundle. In the Hilbert case, given a tuple of integers
(k_{i},l_{i})_{1\leq i\leq f}
such that
k_{i}+2l_{i}
equals a constant
c
for all
i
, the corresponding automorphic line bundle of weight
\lambda=(k_{i},l_{i})_{i}
is given by:

\mathcal{L}(\lambda)=\bigotimes_{i=1}^{f}\big((\textnormal{det }H_{1}^{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{A}/S_{K}(G))_{i})^{\otimes l_{i}}\otimes\omega_{i}^{\otimes k_{i}}\big).

(3)

In
[
50
]
, the author proved the following criterion (We omit the issue of compactification for simplicity)

Theorem 1.2
.

\mathcal{L}(\lambda)
is ample if and only if
pk_{i}>k_{i+1}
for
1\leq i\leq f
.

Theorem 1.3
.

\mathcal{L}(\lambda)
is nef if and only if
pk_{i}\geq k_{i+1}
for
1\leq i\leq f
.

As a corollary, we obtain a Kodaira type vanishing result in characteristic
p
:

Corollary 1.4
.

Let
S_{K}(G)^{\textnormal{tor}}
be the toroidal compactification (for a given smooth admissible polyhedral cone decomposition, as in
[
33
]
) of
S_{K}(G)
and
D=S_{K}(G)^{\textnormal{tor}}\backslash S_{K}(G)
be the boundary divisor. If
\lambda
is an ample weight, then

H^{i}(S_{K}(G)^{\textnormal{tor}},{}^{j}(-D)\otimes\mathcal{L}(\lambda))=0,\qquad\textnormal{for $i+j>\textnormal{sup}\{f,2f-p\}$}.

(4)

However, beyond the Hilbert case,
\mathcal{V}(\lambda)
are usually vector bundles. It is less common to directly discuss the ampleness of
\mathcal{V}(\lambda)
. Instead, we consider a Borel–Weil construction (see, for example,
[
15
]
). Roughly speaking, we define
Y
as the moduli space over
k
that parametrizes tuples
(s,\textnormal{Fil}^{\bullet})
, where

\bullet

s
is a point of
S_{K}(G)
;

\bullet

\textnormal{Fil}^{\bullet}
is a full refinement of the Hodge filtration with respect to additional
G
-structures.

The theory of
G
-zips and
G
-zipflags gives a more natural and more rigorous group-theoretic interpretation of the above construction. See Section 3 for an example. Admitting this moduli interpretation, it follows that the fibers of the natural forgetful map
Y\to S_{K}(G)
are isomorphic to the flag variety
P/B
over
k
. We refer to
Y
as the flag space in this paper.

For any
\lambda\in X^{\ast}_{+,L}
, one can construct a natural line bundle
\mathcal{L}_{Y}(\lambda)
over
Y
, which we will refer to as the
automorphic line bundle
of weight
\lambda
in this paper.

The following result, which is originally due to Borel–Weil and Bott (see
[
15
]
), explains the relation between
\mathcal{L}_{Y}(\lambda)
and
\mathcal{L}_{X}(\lambda)
.

Proposition 1.5
.

Let
\pi:Y\to S_{K}(G)
be the natural projection. For any
\lambda\in X^{\ast}_{+,L}
,

\pi_{\ast}\mathcal{L}_{Y}(\lambda)=\mathcal{V}(\lambda)

(5)

and

H^{i}(Y,\mathcal{L}_{Y}(\lambda))=H^{i}(S_{K}(G),\mathcal{V}(\lambda)).

(6)

Therefore, for our purpose, we should ask the following question instead.

Question:
When is the line bundle
\mathcal{L}_{Y}(\lambda)
ample?

We first review the history of this problem.

1.

In
[
2
]
, Andreatta and Goren computed the ample cone of Hilbert modular surface (
f=2
case of Theorem
1.2
), by constructing partial Hasse invariants and studying intersection theory on the surface directly.

2.

In
[
44
]
, Tian and Xiao conjectured the ampleness criterion for
U(2)
/Hilbert/quaternionic Shimura varieties. They studied the geometric description of Goren–Oort strata and proved the necessity part of Theorem
1.2
.

3.

In
[
50
]
, the author proved the ampleness criterion conjectured by
[
44
]
, by computing the intersection numbers using the description of strata.

4.

In the unpublished paper
[
6
]
, Brunebarbe–Goldring–Koskivirta–Stroh proposed a sufficiency criterion for ampleness for Hodge type Shimura varieties. However, their bound is known to be not sharp (even in the Hilbert case).

Little is known about the ampleness criterion beyond the above cases, especially when the group
G
is not of type
A_{1}
. The main difficulty is that the stratification of the flag space is usually very complicated, and computing intersection numbers
(\mathcal{L}_{Y}(\lambda)\cdot C)
with a complete curve
C\subseteq Y
is subtle.

In this paper, we give a complete answer to the above question when
G_{\mdmathbb{Q}_{p}}^{\textnormal{sc}}
is the unramified Weil restriction of
SL_{n}
.

1.2 Statement of the main results.

Let
F
be a totally real field of degree
N
over
\mdmathbb{Q}
in which
p
is inert. We label the set of real embeddings of
F
by
\{\tau_{1},\dots,\tau_{N}\}
as before. Let
E/F
be an imaginary quadratic extension in which
p\mathcal{O}_{E}=\mathfrak{q}\mathfrak{q}^{c}
. Each
\tau_{i}
extends to complex embeddings
\tilde{\tau}_{i}
(over
\mathfrak{q}
) and
\tilde{\tau}_{i}^{c}
(over
\mathfrak{q}^{c}
). For a tuple of integers
(m_{i},n_{i})_{1\leq i\leq N}
such that
m_{i}+n_{i}=n
, let
G
be the unitary similitude group over
\mdmathbb{Q}
, with signatures at infinite places given by
(m_{i},n_{i})_{1\leq i\leq N}
, i.e.,

G(\mdmathbb{R})=G\big(\prod_{i=1}^{N}U(m_{i},n_{i})\big).

(7)

Let
X
be the special fiber of the corresponding Shimura variety of signature
(m_{i},n_{i})
. We apologize for using the letter
X
again (but for a different meaning) to maintain consistency with the rest of the paper. The signature condition implies that, in the Hodge filtration,

0\longrightarrow\omega_{\mathcal{A}^{\vee}/X,\tilde{\tau}_{i}}\longrightarrow H_{1}^{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{A}/X)_{\tilde{\tau}_{i}}\longrightarrow\textnormal{Lie}_{\mathcal{A}/X,\tilde{\tau}_{i}}\longrightarrow 0,

(8)

\omega_{\mathcal{A}^{\vee}/X,\tilde{\tau}_{i}}
(resp.
\omega_{\mathcal{A}^{\vee}/X,\tilde{\tau}_{i}^{c}}
) is a subbundle of
\omega_{\mathcal{A}^{\vee}/X}
of rank
m_{i}
(resp.
n_{i}
). The polarization in the moduli problem induces a perfect pairing

\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle:H_{1}^{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{A}/X)_{\tilde{\tau}_{i}}\times H_{1}^{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{A}/X)_{\tilde{\tau}_{i}^{c}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{O}_{X}

(9)

such that
\omega_{\mathcal{A}^{\vee}/X,\tilde{\tau}_{i}^{c}}=\omega_{\mathcal{A}^{\vee}/X,\tilde{\tau}_{i}}^{\perp}
under this pairing.

We define the flag space
Y
as the moduli space over
k
whose
S
-valued points are
(s,\textnormal{Fil}^{\bullet})
, where
s\in X(S)
, and
\textnormal{Fil}=\{\textnormal{Fil}_{i},\textnormal{Fil}_{i}^{c}\}
is a collection of filtrations at each place:

\textnormal{Fil}_{i}:0=\mathcal{F}^{i}_{0}\subseteq\mathcal{F}^{i}_{1}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq\mathcal{F}^{i}_{m_{i}-1}\subseteq\mathcal{F}^{i}_{m_{i}}=\omega_{A^{\vee}/S,\tilde{\tau}_{i}}\subseteq\mathcal{F}^{i}_{m_{i}+1}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq\mathcal{F}^{i}_{n-1}\subseteq\mathcal{F}^{i}_{n}=H_{1}^{\mathrm{dR}}(A/S)_{\tau_{i}}.

(10)

Similarly for the
\tilde{\tau}_{i}^{c}
-part. In addition, we require that
\mathcal{F}^{i^{c}}_{u}=\mathcal{F}^{i,\perp}_{n-u}
for
1\leq i\leq N
and
0\leq u\leq n
. Here,
\mathcal{F}^{i^{c}}_{u}
is a vector bundle in the
\tilde{\tau}_{i}^{c}
-component. Since there are dual relations between the
\tilde{\tau}_{i}
-and the
\tilde{\tau}_{i}^{c}
-parts, we can simplify the data by only recording the refinement of the Hodge filtration at all the
\tilde{\tau}_{i}
-components.

In general, irreducible automorphic vector bundles are parametrized by a character in
X^{\ast}_{+,L}
, i.e., a character of
T
that is dominant with respect to
L
. For our specific
G
, a character
\lambda\in X^{\ast}_{+,L}
corresponds to a tuple of integers
\{k^{i}_{j}\}^{1\leq i\leq N}_{1\leq j\leq n}
. The automorphic line bundle of weight
\lambda
is

\mathcal{L}_{Y}(\lambda)=\bigotimes_{i=1}^{N}\bigotimes_{j=1}^{n}\big(\mathcal{F}^{i}_{j}/\mathcal{F}^{i}_{j-1}\big)^{\otimes k^{i}_{j}}.

(11)

The main theorem of this paper is

Theorem 1.6
.

[Theorem
3.24
,
3.25
,
3.26
,
3.27
]
Let
T=\{i|m_{i}\notin\{0,n\}\}
and
t=\#T
.

1.

If
t=0
, then
X
is a Shimura set, and
Y
is a disjoint union of flag varieties in characteristic
p
.
\mathcal{L}_{Y}(\lambda)
is ample if and only if

k^{i}_{n}>k^{i}_{n-1}>\cdots>k^{i}_{1},\qquad\textnormal{for $1\leq i\leq N$}.

(12)

2.

If
t=1
, we assume
T=\{1\}
for simplicity. If moreover
m_{1}=n-1
, then
\mathcal{L}_{Y}(\lambda)
is ample if and only if

\displaystyle k^{1}_{n-1}>k^{1}_{n-2}>\cdots>k^{1}_{1},

(13)

\displaystyle k^{i}_{n}>k^{i}_{n-1}>\cdots>k^{i}_{1},\qquad\textnormal{for $2\leq i\leq N$},

\displaystyle\big(\sum_{u=0}^{n-2}p^{Nu}\big)\sum_{j=0}^{N-1}\frac{1}{p^{j}}(k_{1}^{1+j}-k^{1+j}_{n})>\sum_{r=2}^{n-1}p^{(r-2)N}\sum_{j=0}^{N-1}\frac{1}{p^{j}}(k^{1+j}_{r}-k^{1+j}_{n}).

3.

If
t=1
, we assume
T=\{1\}
for simplicity. If moreover
m_{1}=1
, then
\mathcal{L}_{Y}(\lambda)
is ample if and only if

\displaystyle k^{1}_{n}>k^{1}_{n-1}>\cdots>k^{1}_{2},

(14)

\displaystyle k^{i}_{n}>k^{i}_{n-1}>\cdots>k^{i}_{1},\qquad\qquad\textnormal{for $i\neq 1$},

\displaystyle\big(\sum_{u=0}^{n-2}p^{Nu}\big)\sum_{j=0}^{N-1}\frac{1}{p^{j}}(k_{1}^{1+j}-k^{1+j}_{n})>\sum_{r=2}^{n-1}p^{(n-1-r)N}\sum_{j=0}^{N-1}\frac{1}{p^{j}}(k^{1+j}_{1}-k^{1+j}_{r}).

4.

In all other cases, let
T=\{i_{1},\dots,i_{t}\}
with
i_{1}<i_{2}<\cdots<i_{t}
. Let
a(i_{l})=i_{l+1}-i_{l}
for
1\leq l\leq t-1
and
a(i_{t})=i_{1}+N-i_{t}
. Then
\mathcal{L}_{Y}(\lambda)
is ample if and only if

\displaystyle k^{i_{l}}_{m_{i_{l}}}>k^{i_{l}}_{m_{i_{l}}-1}>\cdots>k^{i_{l}}_{1},\quad k^{i_{l}}_{n}>k^{i_{l}}_{n-1}>\cdots>k^{i_{l}}_{m_{i_{l}}+1},\qquad\textnormal{for all $1\leq l\leq t$},

(15)

\displaystyle k^{j}_{n}>k^{j}_{n-1}>\cdots>k^{j}_{1},\qquad\textnormal{for $j\notin T$},

\displaystyle p^{a(i_{l})}\sum_{j=0}^{a(i_{l})-1}\frac{1}{p^{j}}(k^{i_{l}+j}_{1}-k^{i_{1}+j}_{n})>k^{i_{l+1}}_{m_{i_{l+1}}}-k^{i_{l+1}}_{m_{i_{l+1}}+1}\qquad\textnormal{for all $1\leq l\leq t$.}

Moreover,
\mathcal{L}_{Y}(\lambda)
is nef if and only if we substitute all the ">" by
\geq
in the above inequalities.

Note that the formulas are uniform in general, except the case
t=1
.

We remark that the notion of flag space can be generalized to partial flag spaces, by recording a partial refinement of the Hodge filtration. There are analogous theorems for partial flag spaces over
X
. In fact, the proof of Theorem
1.6
is essentially assembling these results over partial flag spaces (See Theorem
3.28
,
3.29
,
3.32
in Section 3).

Before sketching the proof of Theorem
1.6
, we first discuss some applications of this ampleness criterion to other topics.

1.3 Applications.

1.3.1 Vanishing of coherent cohomology.

As we mentioned earlier, the study of different cohomology theories plays an important role in the world of Shimura varieties. There have been studies
[
9
,
10
,
12
,
20
]
, etc, to control the (concentration of) étale cohomology of Shimura varieties,
[
1
,
31
,
32
]
, etc, to establish the vanishing results of coherent cohomology. Our ampleness criterion directly yields the Kodaira-type vanishing results.

Theorem 1.7
.

Set
d=\textnormal{dim $Y$}
and let
\mathcal{L}_{Y}(\lambda)
be an ample line bundle over
Y
. Then

H^{i}(Y,{}^{j}_{\mathcal{A}/Y}\otimes\mathcal{L}_{Y}(\lambda))=0,\qquad\textnormal{for $i+j>\textnormal{max$\{d,2d-p\}$}$}.

(16)

We can also establish new vanishing results on
X
. When
\lambda
is a character of
L\subseteq P
, the irreducible representation of highest weight
\lambda
is 1-dimensional. Consequently, the automorphic vector bundle
\mathcal{V}(\lambda)
over
X
is locally free of rank one. We sometimes refer to it as
\mathcal{L}_{X}(\lambda)
. In our unitary setup, such
\lambda
corresponds to a tuple of integers
\{k_{i}\}_{i=1}^{N}
, and

\mathcal{L}_{X}(\lambda)=\mathcal{L}_{X}(\{k_{i}\})=\bigotimes_{i=1}^{N}\big(\textnormal{det }\omega_{\mathcal{A}^{\vee}/X,\tau_{i}}\big)^{\otimes k_{i}}.

(17)

The proof of Theorem
1.6
will also imply the following criterion for
\mathcal{L}_{X}(\lambda)
:

Theorem 1.8
.

[Theorem
3.29
]
If
T=\{i_{1},\dots,i_{t}\}
. The line bundle
\mathcal{L}_{X}(\{k_{i}\})
is ample if and only if

p^{a(i_{l})}k_{i_{l}}>k_{i_{l+1}},\qquad\textnormal{for $1\leq l\leq t$}.

(18)

Recall the Kodaira type vanishing result in
[
31
]
:

Theorem 1.9
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{L}_{X}(\{k_{i}\})
is ample, and
\mu\in X^{+,<_{\textnormal{re}}p}_{G}
. Then for any
w\in W^{M}
, we have

H^{i-l(w)}(X,\mathcal{L}_{X}(\lambda)\otimes\mathcal{V}^{\vee}({w\cdot\mu}))=0,\qquad\textnormal{for $i>d$}.

(19)

We omit the detailed explanation of the notations here as they are beyond the scope of this paper. In Lan and Suh’s original paper, they applied this theorem together with the ampleness of the Hodge line bundle
\textnormal{det }\omega
(which corresponds to
\mathcal{L}_{X}(\{1,\dots,1\})
) to deduce their vanishing results. We could use the ampleness criterion in this paper to improve this vanishing result for unitary Shimura varieties.

1.3.2 Applications to mod
p
automorphic forms.

A direct application of the vanishing results of coherent cohomology is to understand the congruences of
p
-adic automorphic forms and liftability of mod
p
automorphic forms, or more generally, cohomological classes. This approach has been used in
[
14
,
4
,
17
]
, to construct Galois representations attached to cohomology classes.

In our joint work in progress
[
49
]
, we prove the following conjecture by Diamond and Sasaki in
[
13
]
.

Conjecture 1.10
.

Let
\overline{\rho}:G_{F}\to GL_{2}(\overline{\mdmathbb F}_{p})
be an irreducible mod
p
Galois representation. Then
\overline{\rho}
is
algebraically modular
of weight
(\underline{k},\underline{l})
if and only if
\overline{\rho}
is
geometrically modular
of weight
(\underline{k},\underline{l})
.

For the definitions of two notions of modularity, see
[
7
]
and
[
13
]
. In fact, we generalize the statement to quaternionic Shimura varieties and prove the conjecture under some mild Taylor–Wiles hypotheses. The ampleness criterion is a first input to deduce the liftability of mod
p
automorphic forms of certain weights. We also apply the geometric Jacquet–Langlands correspondence developed in this paper to relate automorphic forms for different quaternionic Shimura varieties. This convinces us that the ampleness criterion in this paper can provide a potential method in attacking the modularity of mod
p
representations beyond the
GL_{2}
case.

1.4Ingredients of the proof.

1.4.1 Geometry of the special fiber and a geometric Jacquet–Langlands correspondence.

Let
X
denote the special fiber of a Hodge type Shimura variety of hyperspecial level at
p
. There exists a structure morphism

\zeta:X\longrightarrow G\textnormal{-zip}^{\mu}.

(20)

The target of this morphism is called the stack of
G
-zips. This is a group-theoretical object first introduced by Moonen and Wedhorn in
[
39
,
38
]
for
G=GL_{n}
, and systematically studied by Pink, Wedhorn and Ziegler for a general reductive group
G
in
[
52
,
42
]
. The stack of
G
-zips captures the structure of the mod
p
de Rham cohomology of the universal abelian scheme and its Frobenius structure.

The stack of
G
-zips admits a stratification

G\textnormal{-zip}^{\mu}=\coprod_{w\in{{}^{I}}W}G\textnormal{-zip}^{\mu}_{w}.

Zhang showed that the morphism
\zeta
is smooth in
[
51
]
, so we may pullback the stratification to X. The resulting stratification on
X
is called the
Ekedahl–Oort stratification
.

There have been extensive studies on the geometry of Ekedahl–Oort stratification (See
[
16
,
18
,
29
]
,
[
17
,
46
,
47
]
, etc). We note that while the Ekedahl–Oort strata are smooth themselves, their closures are generally highly singular. We will also study the normalization of some Ekedahl–Oort strata.

The crucial geometric aspect of our proof, namely, a geometric mod
p
Jacquet–Langlands correspondence, deserves discussion. In
[
24
]
, Helm studied the sparse Goren–Oort strata of
U(2)
-Shimura varieties and proved that each sparse stratum is a
\mdmathbb{P}^{1}
-power bundle over another
U(2)
Shimura variety with different signature. Subsequent works of
[
44
,
23
]
employed Helm’s trick to provide a comprehensive description of all Goren–Oort strata in Hilbert/quaternionic/
U(2)
Shimura varieties. The work in
[
48
]
significantly extended this description of strata to the basic locus of Hodge type Shimura varieties. They established a parametrization of the basic locus by another Shimura variety with different signature, where the fibers are isomorphic to iterated Deligne–Lusztig varieties.

In this paper, rather than using this
\mdmathbb{P}^{1}
-bundle description, we conceptualize this phenomenon as a correspondence between the (partial) flag spaces of
different
unitary Shimura varieties. The main theorem for this step is

Proposition 1.11

(Theorem
4.6
)
.

Let
B\subseteq P_{0}\subseteq P
be an intermediate parabolic subgroup such that the associated partial flag space
Y_{P_{0}}
is characterized by recording precisely one subbundle
\mathcal{E}_{r_{i}}^{i}\subseteq\omega_{i}
of rank
r_{i}
for each
i
. Let
Z
denote the closed subscheme of
Y_{P_{0}}
cut out by the condition

\mathcal{E}^{i}_{r_{i}}\subseteq V^{-1}(\mathcal{E}_{r_{i+1}}^{i+1,(p)}),\qquad F(\mathcal{E}_{r_{i+1}}^{i+1,(p)})\subseteq\mathcal{E}^{i}_{r_{i}}

(21)

for all
i
. Then
Z
is, up to some Frobenius factor, isomorphic to a stratum
Z^{\prime}
in some partial flag space
Y^{\prime}_{P_{0}^{\prime}}
over
X^{\prime}
, where
X^{\prime}
is the special fiber of a unitary Shimura variety with hyperspecial level structure and signature condition

m_{i}^{\prime}=m_{i}-r_{i}+r_{i-1},\qquad\textnormal{for $1\leq i\leq N$}.

(22)

Furthermore, this correspondence respects extra structures.

This correspondence can be understood through the following diagram
W
Z
Z^{\prime}
Y_{P_{0}}
Y^{\prime}_{P_{0}^{\prime}}
X
X^{\prime}
pr_{1}
\sim
pr_{2}
\sim
\supseteq
\subseteq
\pi
\pi

Here
X
and
X^{\prime}
are unitary Shimura varieties with different signatures,
Y_{P_{0}}
and
Y_{P_{0}^{\prime}}^{\prime}
are partial flag spaces over
X
and
X^{\prime}
,
Z
and
Z^{\prime}
are strata within
Y_{P_{0}}
and
Y_{P_{0}^{\prime}}^{\prime}
respectively. The symbol
\sim
stands for an isomorphism up to Frobenius twists. This diagram allows us to relate automorphic line bundles over
different
unitary Shimura varieties via pullback, allowing an inductive proof on possible signatures of the unitary group.

A geometric Jacquet–Langlands correspondence of such kind has its application in various arithmetic problems: In
[
23
,
48
]
for the construction of Tate cycles; In
[
12
]
to avoid trace formula computations; In our work in progress
[
49
]
to transform modularity of mod
p
Galois representations.

1.4.2 Strata Hasse invariants.

The classical Hasse invariant
h
is a modular form of weight
p-1
in characteristic
p
, induced by the Verschiebung map on
\omega
. It has
q
-expansion equal to 1. The zero locus of
h
is the supersingular locus.

It is natural to consider generalizations of the Hasse invariant to higher-dimensional Shimura varieties. By a
strata Hasse invariant
, we mean a section
s\in H^{0}(\overline{X_{w}},\mathcal{V}(\lambda))
for some
w\in{{}^{I}}W
and
\lambda\in X^{\ast}_{+,L}
, such that
Z(s)
is a union of strata. In
[
2
]
, partial Hasse invariants were constructed for Hilbert modular varieties. In recent years, various generalizations have been developed (from different perspectives, though those related to the theory of
G
-zips are most relevant to our paper), see, for example,
[
4
,
43
,
28
,
25
,
16
,
17
]
. In the work of
[
14
,
4
,
17
]
, they constructed strata Hasse invariants and used them to study congruences of automorphic forms.

To prove the ampleness criterion, our naive idea is to express the class
[\mathcal{L}_{Y}(\lambda)]
as a non-negative linear combination of strata Hasse invariants. Then the intersection number
(\mathcal{L}_{Y}(\lambda)\cdot C)
would be non-negative as long as the test curve
C
does not lie in any lower-dimensional strata. Then we can reduce the problem to a lower dimensional variety and continue this process. To avoid strata with bad geometric properties, we need a more careful construction of strata Hasse invariants whose zero locus does not contain these specific strata. This is a generalization of the previous work, which relies on the moduli interpretation.

1.4.3 Outline of the proof and structure of the paper.

In Section 2 we define unitary Shimura varieties. However, since our focus is on the local structure at
p
and the signature at infinite places of the Shimura variety, the group
G
can be replaced by any other unitary group over
\mdmathbb{Q}
that differs from
G
at finite places away from
p
. The result in this paper applies to these compact unitary Shimura varieties.

In Section 3, we introduce the stack of
G
-zips and the Ekedahl–Oort stratification of
X
. We also review the construction of (partial) flag spaces
Y_{P^{\prime}}
over
X
and their stratifications. We then provide an explicit construction of the automorphic line bundles
\mathcal{L}_{P^{\prime}}(\lambda)
. We consider all possible line bundles over all partial flag spaces (including the case
P=P^{\prime}
, in which
Y_{P^{\prime}}=X
) simultaneously. We state our main theorem and its variants at the end of the section.

In Section 4, we prove the first key theorem in this paper, namely, the description of strata mentioned above. The goal of this section is to establish the geometric Jacquet–Langlands correspondence for certain strata of the flag spaces associated to different unitary Shimura varieties.

In Section 5, we prove the necessity part of Theorem
1.6
. The idea is straightforward: Since
\mathcal{L}_{Y}(\lambda)
is ample, its restriction to any closed subscheme of
Y
must remain ample. For case (1) in Theorem
1.6
(1), we deduce the inequalities from the theory of flag varieties; For case (2) and (3) of Theorem
1.6
, we select a special curve
C\subseteq Y
and restricting to
C
yields the desired result; For the remaining case, we choose a stratum
Z\subseteq Y
whose dimension is strictly larger than 1 and which admits a geometric description. The key is to construct a (highly nontrivial) morphism
Z\to Z^{\prime}
whose fibers are flag varieties. Restricting to the fibers of this morphism yields the desired inequality.

In Section 6, we introduce the notion of slopes. This definition provides a combinatorical understanding of different strata in the flag space.

In Section 7, we prove the sufficiency part of Theorem
1.6
. The proof is lengthy and involved, but the idea is very basic: First, we reduce the proof of the ampleness criterion to the nefness criterion, then we further reduce the problem to showing that the line bundles
\mathcal{L}_{Y^{i}_{j}}(\{k^{i}_{j}\};\alpha)
are nef under certain numerical conditions (See Section 7 for the explicit definitions). To achieve this, we express the class
[\mathcal{L}_{Y^{i}_{j}}(\{k_{i}\};\alpha)]
in the rational Picard group as a non-negative linear combination

[\mathcal{L}_{Y^{i}_{j}}(\{k^{i}_{j}\};\alpha)]=\sum_{l=1}^{l_{0}}u_{l}[h_{l}]+[\mathcal{L}_{0}],

(23)

where
h_{l}
’s are strata Hasse invariants, and
\mathcal{L}_{0}
is a line bundle (obtained as a pullback under a proper morphism) that is nef by induction. This shows that

\big([\mathcal{L}_{Y^{i}_{j}}(\{k^{i}_{j}\};\alpha)]\cdot C\big)\geq 0

(24)

as long as
C
is not contained in the zero locus of any
h_{l}
. We can thus reduce the problem to a stratum of lower dimension. However, the actual construction is highly combinatorical and requires induction on some variants. We will give a more detailed overview at the beginning of Section 7.

2  Unitary Shimura varieties

Remark 2.1
.

The main result of this paper is purely local, and the structure of the reductive group
G
at places away from
p
and
\infty
is not really important for us. We use the following moduli interpretation for simplicity. The result also works for other moduli problems, as long as the unitary group satisfies the condition that
G_{\mdmathbb{Q}_{p}}
is isomorphic to the Weil restriction of
GL_{n}
of a totally real field and the resulting Shimura variety is
compact
. For example, the moduli problem in
[
30
]
.

2.1 Notations.

Let
p
be a prime number. We fix an isomorphism
\iota:\mdmathbb{C}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\overline{\mdmathbb{Q}}_{p}
. Let
F
be a totally real field of degree
N
over
\mdmathbb{Q}
in which
p
is inert and
\mathfrak{p}
be the unique prime above
p
. We denote by
\Sigma
the set of places of
F
and
\Sigma_{\infty}
the subset of real embeddings. Through the isomorphism
\iota
, we view the set
\Sigma_{\infty}
also as the set of
p
-adic embeddings
F\hookrightarrow\overline{\mdmathbb{Q}}_{p}
, and we can label
\Sigma_{\infty}
by
\tau_{1},\dots,\tau_{N}
, such that

\sigma^{-1}\tau_{i}=\tau_{i+1},

(25)

where
\sigma
is the Frobenius endomorphism.

Let
E/F
be a CM extension such that
\mathfrak{p}\mathcal{O}_{E}=\mathfrak{q}\mathfrak{q}^{c}
. We fix the choice of a prime
\mathfrak{q}
over
\mathfrak{p}
. Let
\Sigma_{E,\infty}
be the set of complex embeddings of
E
and view them as
p
-adic embeddings as above. For each
\tau_{i}\in\Sigma_{\infty}
, let
\tilde{\tau}_{i}
and
\tilde{\tau}^{c}_{i}
be the embeddings of
E
that extend
\tau_{i}
and correspond to
\mathfrak{q}
and
\mathfrak{q}^{c}
respectively.

Set
\kappa=\mdmathbb{F}_{p^{N}}
and
k=\overline{\mdmathbb{F}}_{p}
. The inclusion
\kappa\hookrightarrow k
induces an embedding of the ring of Witt vectors
W(\kappa)
into
\overline{\mdmathbb{Q}}_{p}
.

2.2 The Shimura datum.

Let
n\geq 2
be an integer and
\{(m_{i},n_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq N}
be a tuple of non-negative integers such that
m_{i}+n_{i}=n
for all
1\leq i\leq N
. We will define the compact unitary Shimura variety of good reduction of signature
\{(m_{i},n_{i})\}_{1\leq i\leq N}
in the following.

•

Choose an
2n
-dimensional
E
-vector space
V
equipped with a nondegenerate alternating pairing
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle:V\times V\longrightarrow\mdmathbb{Q}
satisfying

\langle\alpha x,y\rangle=\langle x,\bar{\alpha}y\rangle,\quad\alpha\in E,

(26)

and the following signature condition: For each
\tau\in\Sigma_{\infty}
, the pairing
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
induces an alternating pairing on the complex vector space
V_{\tau}=V\otimes_{F,\tau}\mdmathbb{R}
. This pairing is always the “imaginary” part of a uniquely determined Hermitian form
[\cdot,\cdot]_{\tau_{i}}
on
V_{\tau_{i}}
. We require the signature of
[\cdot,\cdot]_{\tau_{i}}
to be
(m_{i},n_{i})
.

•

We define
G
to be the unitary similitude group of
V
. Concretely, for a
\mdmathbb{Q}
-algebra
R
,

G(R)=\{(g,c(g))\in\mathrm{Aut}_{E\otimes{Q}R}(V\otimes_{\mdmathbb{Q}}R)\times R^{\times},\ \langle gx,gy\rangle=c(g)\langle x,y\rangle,\ \forall x,y\in V\otimes_{\mdmathbb{Q}}R\}.

(27)

It is easy to see that

G(\mdmathbb{R})=\mathrm{G}\bigg(\prod_{1\leq i\leq N}\mathrm{U}(m_{i},n_{i})\bigg).

(28)

•

We consider the homomorphism of
\mdmathbb{R}
-algebraic groups
h:\mdmathbb{S}=\rm{Res}_{\mdmathbb{C}/\mdmathbb{R}}\mdmathbb{G}_{m}\to G{R}
defined by

h(z)=\prod_{1\leq i\leq N}\left(\begin{matrix}z&&&&&\\
&\ddots&&&&\\
&&z&&&\\
&&&\bar{z}&&\\
&&&&\ddots&\\
&&&&&\bar{z}\end{matrix}\right),

(29)

where on the diagonal for each
i
,
z
appears with multiplicity
m_{i}
and
\bar{z}
appears with multiplicity
n_{i}
. Here, the embedding
\textnormal{U}(m_{i},n_{i})\subseteq\textnormal{GL}_{n}(\mdmathbb{C})
is induced by
\tau_{i}
. Let
X
be the
G(\mdmathbb{R})
-conjugacy class of
h
.

The above defines a Shimura datum
(G,X)
.

2.3 Moduli interpretation.

Assume
V_{\mdmathbb{Q}_{p}}
admits a self dual lattice. Let
K=K^{p}K_{p}\subseteq G(\mdmathbb{A}{Q}^{f})
be an open subgroup with hyperspecial level structure at
p
. In other words,
G
extends to a reductive group scheme
\mathcal{G}
over
\mdmathbb{Z}_{p}
with
\mathcal{G}\otimes_{\mdmathbb{Z}_{p}}\mdmathbb{Q}_{p}\simeq G_{\mdmathbb{Q}_{p}}
and
K_{p}=\mathcal{G}(\mdmathbb{Z}_{p})
. For
K^{p}
sufficiently small, the Shimura variety
Sh_{K}(G)
can be realized as the moduli scheme representating the functor:

Sch^{\textnormal{loc noe}}_{/W(k)}\longrightarrow Sets

(30)

sending a locally noetherian scheme
S
over
W(k)
to the set
Sh_{K}(G_{\mathcal{P}})(S)
consisting of equivalent classes of tuples
(A,\lambda,\eta)
satisfying:

1.

A
is an abelian scheme of dimension
2nN
over
S
equipped with an action of
\mathcal{O}_{E}
, such that the characteristic polynomial of
\alpha\in\mathcal{O}_{E}
on
\omega_{A^{\vee}/S}
is given by

\prod_{1\leq i\leq N}(x-\tilde{\tau_{i}}(\alpha))^{m_{i}}(x-\tilde{\tau_{i}}^{c}(\alpha))^{n_{i}}.

(31)

In other words, if
\omega_{A^{\vee}/S,\tilde{\tau}_{i}}
(resp.
\omega_{A^{\vee}/S,\tilde{\tau}_{i}^{c}}
) denotes the subsheaf of
\omega_{A^{\vee}/S}
on which
\mathcal{O}_{E}
acts via
\tilde{\tau}_{i}
(resp. via
\tilde{\tau}_{i}^{c}
), then it is a subbundle of rank
m_{i}
(resp.
n_{i}
).

2.

\lambda:A\to A^{\vee}
is a prime-to-
p
quasi-polarization of
A
such that the Rosati involution associated with
\lambda
induces the complex conjugation on
\mathcal{O}_{E}
.

3.

\eta
is a
K^{p}
-level structure on
A
. Explicitly, this means that
\eta
is a collection of
\pi_{1}(S_{j},\bar{s}_{j})
-stable
K^{p}
-orbit of isomorphisms
\eta_{j}:V\otimes{Q}\mdmathbb{A}^{(p)}_{f}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}V^{(p)}(A_{\bar{s}_{j}})
for each connected component
S_{j}
of
S
with a geometric point
\bar{s}_{j}
. Here
V^{(p)}(A_{\bar{s}_{j}})=\varprojlim\limits_{p\nmid N}(A_{\bar{s}_{j}}[N])\otimes{Z}\mdmathbb{Q}
is the rational Tate module away from
p
. We further require that for some isomorphism
\nu(\eta_{j})\in\text{Hom}({\mdmathbb{A}}_{f}^{(p)},{\mdmathbb{A}}_{f}^{(p)}(1))
, the following diagram is commutative:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V\otimes_{\mdmathbb{Q}}\mdmathbb{A}_{f}^{(p)}\times V\otimes_{\mdmathbb{Q}}\mdmathbb{A}_{f}^{(p)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mdmathbb{A}}_{f}^{(p)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V^{(p)}(A_{\bar{s}_{j}})\times V^{(p)}(A_{\bar{s}_{j}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mdmathbb{A}}_{f}^{(p)}(1).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu(\eta_{j})}
Weil pairing

Two triples
(A,\lambda,\eta)
and
(A^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime},\eta^{\prime})
are equivalent if there is an
\mathcal{O}_{E}
-equivariant prime-to-
p
quasi-isogeny
\phi:A\to A^{\prime}
such that
\phi^{\vee}\circ\lambda^{\prime}\circ\phi=\lambda
and
\eta^{\prime}=\phi\circ\eta
.

For the rest of the paper, we denote by
X=Sh_{K}(G)_{k}
the geometric special fiber of the Shimura variety. We omit the level structure for simplicity.

3
G
-zips and stratification of the special fiber

In this section, we recall the notion of
G
-zips introduced by Pink, Wedhorn and Ziegler in
[
52
]
. The theory of
G
-zips provides a group-theoretical description of the Ekedahl–Oort stratification on the special fiber of Shimura varieties. We also recall some relevant results by Goldring, Koskivirta, Imai, Wedhorn, etc.

3.1 Notations.

Let
p
be a prime number. Let
k=\overline{\mdmathbb{F}}_{p}
be the algebraic closure of
\mdmathbb{F}_{p}
as above. Let
G
be a connected reductive group over
\mdmathbb{F}_{p}
. For a scheme
X
over
\mdmathbb{F}_{p}
, we denote by
X^{(p)}
its
p
-th power Frobenius twist and by
\varphi:X\to X^{(p)}
the relative Frobenius morphism. Let
(B,T)
be a Borel pair of
G
, meaning that
B\subseteq G
is a Borel subgroup and
T\subseteq B
is a maximal torus of
G
. We will use the following notations:

\bullet

X^{\ast}(T)
(resp.
X_{\ast}(T)
) denotes the group of characters of
T
;

\bullet

W=W(G_{k},T)=N_{G}(T)/T
denotes the Weyl group of
G
;

\bullet

(\Phi,\Phi^{+},\Delta)
is the root system attached to the Borel pair
(B,T)
, i.e.,
\Phi\subseteq X^{\ast}(T)
is the set of
T
-roots in
G
,
\Phi^{+}\subseteq\Phi
is the set of positive roots with respect to
B
, and
\Delta\subseteq\Phi^{+}
is the set of simple roots;

\bullet
For
\alpha\in\Phi
, let
s_{\alpha}\in W
be the corresponding simple reflection. Let
\ell:W\to\mdmathbb{N}
be the length function on
W
. We endow
W
with the Bruhat order:
u\leq v
if and only if
v
can be written in the form
v=s_{\alpha_{1}}\cdots s_{\alpha_{\ell(v)}}
such that
u
has a presentation of the form
s_{\alpha_{i_{1}}}\cdots s_{\alpha_{i_{\ell(u)}}}
with
i_{1}<i_{2}<\cdots<i_{\ell(u)}
.

\bullet
For a subset
I\subseteq\Delta
, let
W_{I}
be the subgroup of
W
generated by
\{s_{\alpha}|\ \alpha\in I\}
.

\bullet
Let
w_{0}
and
w_{0,I}
denote the longest element in
W
and
W_{I}
respectively.

\bullet
Let
{}^{I}W
(resp.
W^{I}
) be the set of elements
w\in W
of minimal length in the coset
W_{I}w
(resp.
wW_{I}
). Then
{}^{I}W
(resp.
W^{I}
) is a set of representatives of the coset space
W_{I}\backslash W
(resp.
W/W_{I}
). The longest element in
W^{I}
is
w_{0}w_{0,I}
.

\bullet
Let
X^{\ast}_{+}(T)
be the set of dominant characters, i.e., characters
\lambda\in X^{\ast}(T)
that satisfies
\langle\lambda,\alpha^{\vee}\rangle\geq 0
for all
\alpha\in\Delta
.

\bullet
Let
X^{\ast}_{+,I}(T)
be the set of
I
-dominant characters
, i.e., characters
\lambda\in X^{\ast}(T)
satisfying
\langle\lambda,\alpha^{\vee}\rangle\geq 0
for all
\alpha\in I
.

\bullet
Let
P\subseteq G_{k}
be a parabolic subgroup containing
T
. Then
P
is conjugate in
G_{k}
to a unique standard parabolic subgroup
P_{I}
containing
B
. We say
P
is of type
I_{P}:=I
. Let
\Delta^{P}=\Delta\backslash I
.

3.2 The stack of
G
-zips.

Definition 3.1

(
[
42
,
52
]
)
.

Let
G
be a connected reductive group over
\mdmathbb{F}_{p}
. A
connected zip datum
is a tuple
\mathcal{Z}:=(G,P,L,Q,M,\varphi)
, where

\bullet

P,Q\subseteq G_{k}
are parabolic subgroups.

\bullet

L\subseteq P
and
M\subseteq Q
are Levi subgroups and

\bullet

\varphi:L\to M
is an isogeny.

Given a choice of Levi subgroup
L
(resp.
M
), every element
x\in P
(resp.
y\in Q
) admits a unique decomposition
x=ul
(resp.
y=vm
) where
u\in R_{u}(P)
and
l\in L
(resp.
v\in R_{u}(Q)
and
m\in M
). We define the zip group as

E_{\mathcal{Z}}:=\{(x,y)\in P\times Q|\ \varphi(\bar{x})=\bar{y}\}.

(32)

Here,
\overline{\bullet}
means the Levi factor of
\bullet
. The zip group
E_{\mathcal{Z}}
acts on
G
via

(x,y)\cdot g:=x^{-1}gy.

(33)

The following example provides a typical construction of zip data.

Example 3.2
.

By a cocharacter datum, we mean a pair
(G,\mu)
, where
G
is a connected reductive group over
\mdmathbb{F}_{p}
and
\mu:G_{m,k}\to G_{k}
is a cocharacter. A cocharacter datum gives rise to a connected zip datum
\mathcal{Z}_{\mu}=(G,P,Q,L,M,\varphi)
as follows:

\bullet

Q:=(P^{+})^{(p)}
, where
P^{+}
is the parabolic subgroup containing
B
such that

P^{+}(k)=\{g\in G(k)\ |\ \lim_{t\to 0}\ \mu(t)g\mu(t)^{-1}\textnormal{ exists}\}.

(34)

\bullet

P:=P^{-}
is the opposite parabolic subgroup of
P^{+}
.

\bullet

L:=P^{-}\cap P^{+}=\textnormal{Cent($\mu$)}
and
M:=L^{(p)}
.

\bullet

\varphi:L\to M
is the relative Frobenius morphism.

We call this tuple a
connected zip datum of type
(G,\mu)
.

Definition 3.3
.

Let
S
be a
k
-scheme and
\mathcal{Z}
a connected zip datum. A
G
-zip of type
\mathcal{Z}
over
S
is a tuple
\underline{I}=(I,I_{P},I_{Q},\psi)
where
I
is a
G
-torsor over
S
,
I_{P}\subseteq I
is a
P
-torsor,
I_{Q}\subseteq I
is a
Q
-torsor, and
\psi:(I_{P}/R_{u}P)^{(p)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}I_{Q}/R_{u}Q
is an isomorphism of
M
-torsors.

The stack of
G
-zips, denoted by
G\textnormal{-zip}^{\mathcal{Z}}
, is defined to be the classifying stack for
G
-zips of type
\mathcal{Z}
over
k
. If
\mathcal{Z}
arises from a cocharacter datum
(G,\mu)
, we simply denote this stack by
G\textnormal{-zip}^{\mu}
. Pink, Wedhorn and Ziegler proved the following in
[
52
]
:

Theorem 3.4
.

G\textnormal{-zip}^{\mathcal{Z}}
is a smooth algebraic stack of dimension 0 over
k
, and

G\textnormal{-zip}^{\mathcal{Z}}\simeq[E_{\mathcal{Z}}\backslash G].

(35)

Definition 3.5
.

Let
\mathcal{Z}
be a connected zip datum. We call a triple
(B,T,z)
a
frame
for
\mathcal{Z}
if
(B,T)
is a Borel pair of
G_{k}
defined over
\mdmathbb{F}_{p}
and
z\in W
, such that

B\subseteq P,\qquad^{z}B\subseteq Q,\qquad\varphi(B\cap L)=B\cap M={{}^{z}}B\cap M.

(36)

Note that this differs slightly from the original definition in
[
52
]
, where they do not require the Borel pair to be defined over
\mdmathbb{F}_{p}
. Under our definition, a frame does not necessarily exist in general. However, in the case where
\mathcal{Z}=\mathcal{Z}_{\mu}
comes from a cocharacter datum
(G,\mu)
, there exists
g\in G(k)
such that
\mathcal{Z}_{\mu^{\prime}}
where
\mu^{\prime}=\textnormal{ad}_{g}\circ\mu
admits a frame
[
25
]
. For our purpose (of studying stratification and automorphic vector bundles), it is harmless to assume the existence of frames.

The orbit decomposition of
G
with respect to the action of the zip group
E_{\mathcal{Z}}
is actually a stratification.

Theorem 3.6

(
[
52
]
)
.

Let
(B,T,z)
be a frame for
\mathcal{Z}
and
(\Phi,\Phi^{+},\Delta)
be the corresponding root system. Let
I
and
J
be the types of the parabolic subgroups
P
and
Q
, respectively. Then

1. (Orbit decomposition) There are bijections

\displaystyle{}^{I}W\longrightarrow\{E\textnormal{-orbits in $G$}\},

(37)

\displaystyle W^{J}\longrightarrow\{E\textnormal{-orbits in $G$}\}.

The first bijection is given by
w\mapsto o_{\mathcal{Z}}(g\dot{w})
. Here,
\dot{w}
is a lift of
w
to
N_{G}(T)\subseteq G
and
o_{\mathcal{Z}}(\cdot)
represents the orbit of an element. We denote the orbit corresponding to
w\in
{}^{I}W
or
W^{J}
by
G_{w}
.

2. (Dimension)
\textnormal{dim }G_{w}=\ell(w)+\textnormal{dim }P
.

3. (Closure relation) For
w,w^{\prime}\in
{}^{I}W
we write
w^{\prime}\preccurlyeq w
if and only if there exists
y\in W_{I}
such that
y^{-1}w^{\prime}\psi(y)\leq w
. Here,
\psi:W_{I}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\rightarrow}}{W_{J}}
is the morphism induced by
\varphi\circ\textnormal{int}(z):P\to{{}^{z}}Q
on the standard parabolics. We refer to this order as the twisted Bruhat order. Then, for any
w\in^{I}W
,

\overline{G_{w}}=\coprod_{\begin{subarray}{c}v\in^{I}W\\
v\preccurlyeq w\end{subarray}}G_{v}.

(38)

4. (Geometry) Each orbit
G_{w}
is a locally closed smooth subscheme of
G
.

Remark 3.7
.

The closure
\overline{G_{w}}
may be non-smooth. It is even non-normal in general. This lack of smoothness is one of the main difficulties in constructing strata Hasse invariants. In
[
29
]
, the normalization of the closures of strata is studied, and we will use these results in our later discussion.

3.3 The Ekedahl–Oort stratification.

The stack of
G
-zips provides a group-theoretic parametrization of the Ekedahl–Oort stratification of the special fiber of (Hodge type) Shimura varieties, which we now explain. We hope to present a general construction, so we temporarily forget the notations in Section 2 in this subsection.

Let
(G,X)
be a Shimura datum. We assume that the group
G
is unramified at
p
. Then
G
extends to a reductive group
\mathcal{G}
over
\mdmathbb{Z}_{(p)}
such that
\mathcal{G}{Q}=G
. Let
K_{p}:=\mathcal{G}(\mdmathbb{Z}_{p})\subseteq G(\mdmathbb{Q}_{p})
be a hyperspecial subgroup.

Let
E=E(G,X)
be the reflex field of
(G,X)
and let
\mathcal{O}_{E}
denote its ring of integers. Let
\mathfrak{p}
be a prime of
\mathcal{O}_{E}
above
p
. We denote by
E_{\mathfrak{p}}
and
\mathcal{O}_{E,\mathfrak{p}}
the completion of
E
and
\mathcal{O}_{E}
at
\mathfrak{p}
respectively. If
(G,X)
is of abelian type, by the work of Kisin
[
27
]
and Vasiu
[
45
]
, for a sufficiently small open subgroup
K^{p}\subseteq G(\mdmathbb{A}^{p}_{f})
and
K=K^{p}K_{p}
, the Shimura variety
Sh(G,X)_{K}/E
admits a canonical integral model
\mathcal{S}_{K}
defined over
\mathcal{O}_{E,\mathfrak{p}}
. For another compact open subgroup
K^{\prime p}\subseteq G(\mdmathbb{A}^{p}_{f})\subseteq K^{p}
, let
K^{\prime}=K^{\prime p}K_{p}\subseteq K
. The inclusion of levels induces a finite etale projection

\pi_{K^{\prime}/K}:\mathcal{S}_{K^{\prime}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{S}_{K}.

(39)

Let
X=(\mathcal{S}_{K})_{k}
be the geometric special fiber of the integral model, and we omit the level structure for simplicity. Since finite pullbacks of ample line bundles remain ample, this simplification is harmless for the purpose of studying ampleness in this paper.

When the Shimura datum
(G,X)
is of Hodge type, we have the universal abelian scheme
\pi:\mathcal{A}\to X
. The Hodge-to-de Rham spectral sequence degenerates at the
E_{1}
-page, as shown in
[
41
]
. We have the Hodge filtration

\textnormal{Fil}_{H}:\ 0\longrightarrow\omega_{\mathcal{A}/X}\longrightarrow H_{1}^{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{A}/X)\longrightarrow\textnormal{Lie}_{\mathcal{A}/X}\longrightarrow 0

(40)

and the conjugate filtration

\textnormal{Fil}_{\textnormal{conj}}:\ 0\longrightarrow\textnormal{Ker }V\longrightarrow H_{1}^{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{A}/X)\longrightarrow\omega_{\mathcal{A}/X}^{(p)}\longrightarrow 0,

(41)

together with comparison isomorphisms between their subquotients

\displaystyle{}_{1}:\qquad H_{1}^{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{A}/X)/\textnormal{Ker $V$}\xlongrightarrow[\simeq]{V}\omega_{\mathcal{A}^{\vee}/X}^{(p)},

(42)

\displaystyle{}_{2}:\qquad\textnormal{Ker $V$}\xlongrightarrow[\simeq]{F^{-1}}\big(H_{1}^{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{A}/X)/\omega_{\mathcal{A}^{\vee}/X}\big)^{(p)}.

Here,
H_{1}^{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{A}/X):=H^{1}_{\textnormal{dR}}(\mathcal{A}^{\vee}/X)
denotes the de Rham homology of
\mathcal{A}
, and
\omega_{\mathcal{A}/X}=\pi_{\ast}{}_{\mathcal{A}/X}
. Let
\mathcal{Z}_{\mu}=(G,P,Q,L,M,\varphi)
be the zip datum induced by
(G,\mu)
, where
\mu\in X
. The datum
(H_{1}^{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{A}^{\vee}/X),\textnormal{Fil}_{H},\textnormal{Fil}_{\textnormal{conj}},\psi)
induces a morphism

\zeta:X\longrightarrow G\textnormal{-zip}^{\mu}.

(43)

For a more detailed explanation, see
[
17
]
.

Theorem 3.8

(
[
51
]
)
.

The morphism
\zeta
is smooth.

Definition 3.9
.

We define the Ekedahl–Oort stratification (or EO–stratification for short) of
X
as

X=\coprod_{w\in{{}^{I}}W}\zeta^{-1}(G_{w})\qquad\textnormal{or}\qquad X=\coprod_{w\in W^{J}}\zeta^{-1}(G_{w})

(44)

We denote
\zeta^{-1}(G_{w})
by
X_{w}
. By the smoothness of
\zeta
, the EO-stratification has the following properties.

Proposition 3.10
.

1. (Dimension)
\textnormal{dim }X_{w}=\ell(w)
.

2. (Closure relation)

\overline{X_{w}}=\coprod_{\begin{subarray}{c}v\in^{I}W\\
v\preccurlyeq w\end{subarray}}X_{v}.

(45)

3. (Geometry) Each EO–stratum
X_{w}
is a locally closed subvariety of
X
.

Convention:
We will refer to
X_{w}
and
\overline{X_{w}}
as open and closed strata of
X
respectively. By abuse of language, we will refer to both as strata.

Example 3.11
.

We follow the conventions and definitions in Section 2, and explain how to determine the conditions on each EO–stratum in our unitary setup. Fixing the prime
\mathfrak{q}
over
\mathfrak{p}
, the group
G
over
\mdmathbb{F}_{p}
is (non-canonically) isomorphic to
\textnormal{Res}_{\mdmathbb{F}_{p^{N}}/\mdmathbb{F}_{p}}GL_{n,\mdmathbb{F}_{p^{N}}}\times\mdmathbb{G}_{m}
. Thus, the Weyl group
W
is isomorphic to

\prod_{\tau\in\Sigma_{\infty}}\mathcal{S}_{n}.

Here, each
\tau_{i}\in\Sigma_{\infty}
induces an embedding of
\mdmathbb{F}_{p^{N}}
into
k
, and
\mathcal{S}_{n}
is the group of permutations of
n
-elements. The inverse Frobenius
\sigma^{-1}
acts on
W
via

\sigma^{-1}(w_{1},\dots,w_{N})=(w_{2},\dots,w_{N},w_{1}).

(46)

The Levi subgroup
L_{\mu}
of
P=P_{\mu}\subseteq G_{k}
is isomorphic to

\left(\prod_{i=1}^{N}GL_{m_{i}}\times GL_{n_{i}}\right)\times\mdmathbb{G}_{m}.

(47)

Thus, the Weyl group
W_{I}
is isomorphic to the subgroup

\prod_{i=1}^{N}\mathcal{S}_{m_{i}}\times\mathcal{S}_{n_{i}}

(48)

of
W
. The coset

{}^{I}W=\Big\{(w_{1},\dots,w_{N})\in W|w_{i}^{-1}(1)<\cdots<w_{i}^{-1}(m_{i}),\ w_{i}^{-1}(m_{i}+1)<\cdots w_{i}^{-1}(n),\forall 1\leq i\leq N\Big\}.

(49)

For
1\leq i\leq N
, Let
V^{i}
be a standard
k
-vector space of dimension
n
with basis
\{e^{i}_{j}\}_{1\leq j\leq n}
, and let
V=\oplus_{i=1}^{N}V^{i}
. For any
w=(w_{1},\dots,w_{N})\in
{}^{I}W
, we define

\displaystyle V^{i}_{H}:=ke^{i}_{w_{i}^{-1}(1)}\oplus\cdots\oplus ke^{i}_{w_{i}^{-1}(m_{i})},\qquad\textnormal{Fil}^{i,\textnormal{std}}_{H}:0\subseteq V^{i}_{H}\subseteq V^{i}

(50)

\displaystyle V^{i}_{\textnormal{conj}}:=ke^{i}_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus ke^{i}_{n_{i+1}},\qquad\qquad\ \ \textnormal{Fil}^{i,\textnormal{std}}_{\textnormal{conj}}:0\subseteq V^{i}_{\textnormal{conj}}\subseteq V^{i}.

\displaystyle{}^{i,\textnormal{std}}_{1}:V^{i}/V^{i}_{\textnormal{conj}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\longrightarrow}}V^{i+1,(p)}_{H}\quad\qquad\textnormal{given by }{}^{i,\textnormal{std}}_{1}(e^{i}_{n_{i+1}+j})=e^{i+1,(p)}_{w_{i+1}^{-1}(j)},\quad\textnormal{for $1\leq j\leq m_{i+1}$}.

\displaystyle{}^{i,\textnormal{std}}_{2}:V^{i}_{\textnormal{conj}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\longrightarrow}}(V^{i+1}/V^{i+1,(p)}_{H})\quad\ \textnormal{given by }{}^{i,\textnormal{std}}_{2}(e^{i}_{j})=e^{i+1,(p)}_{w_{i+1}^{-1}(m_{i+1}+j)},\quad\textnormal{for $1\leq j\leq n_{i+1}$}.

Here we view the superscripts as modulo
N
. We refer the tuple

\Big(V=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{N}V^{i},\{\textnormal{Fil}^{i,\textnormal{std}}_{H}\},\{\textnormal{Fil}^{i,\textnormal{std}}_{\textnormal{conj}}\},\{{}^{i,\textnormal{std}}_{1},{}^{i,\textnormal{std}}_{2}\}\Big)

(51)

constructed above as a standard
G
-zip of type
\mathcal{Z}_{\mu}
and shape
w
.

By the moduli interpretation in Section 2, the Hodge and conjugate filtrations decompose into

\displaystyle\textnormal{Fil}^{i}_{H}:0\subseteq\omega_{\mathcal{A}^{\vee}/X,\tilde{\tau}_{i}}\subseteq H_{1}^{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{A}/X)_{\tilde{\tau}_{i}},

(52)

\displaystyle\textnormal{Fil}^{i}_{\textnormal{conj}}:0\subseteq\textnormal{Ker $V$}_{\tilde{\tau}_{i}}\subseteq H_{1}^{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{A}/X)_{\tilde{\tau}_{i}},

and the comparison isomorphisms are given by

\displaystyle{}^{i}_{1}:H_{1}^{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{A}/X)_{\tilde{\tau}_{i}}/\textnormal{Ker $V$}_{\tilde{\tau}_{i}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\longrightarrow}}\omega^{(p)}_{\mathcal{A}^{\vee}/X,\tilde{\tau}_{i+1}},

(53)

\displaystyle{}^{i}_{2}:\textnormal{Ker $V$}_{\tilde{\tau}_{i}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\longrightarrow}}(H_{1}^{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{A}/X)_{\tilde{\tau}_{i+1}}/\omega_{\mathcal{A}^{\vee}/X,\tilde{\tau}_{i+1}})^{(p)},

and similarly for the
\tilde{\tau}_{i}^{c}
-components. We can describe the conditions for the Ekedahl–Oort strata of
X
by the proposition below.

Proposition 3.12
.

A closed point
s\in X(k)
lies in
X_{w}(k)
if and only if there is a basis of the
k
-vector space
H_{1}^{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{A}_{k}/k)
under which
(H_{1}^{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{A}_{k}/k),\allowbreak\{\textnormal{Fil}^{i}_{H}\},\allowbreak\{\textnormal{Fil}^{i}_{\textnormal{conj}}\},\{{}^{i}_{1},{}^{i}_{2}\})
is isomorphic to the above standard
G
-zip of type
\mathcal{Z}_{\mu}
and shape
w
.

Remark 3.13
.

1.

A similar construction for
GL_{n}
-zips can be found in the work of Moonen and Wedhorn
[
38
]
. In our paper, we take the conjugate filtration to be the standard filtration. This has benefits when we compute the tangent bundle of certain
\overline{X_{w}}
.

2.

The conditions defining the strata are typically difficult to express explicitly because we must account for the comparison isomorphism
\psi
. This also explains why the closed strata are usually non-normal, unlike Schubert cells, which are given by conditions of the type
\textnormal{dim }\mathcal{M}\cap\mathcal{N}\geq r
.

3.4
G
-zipflags and automorphic bundles

The goal of this subsection is to introduce the flag space, or more generally, partial flag spaces, as well as their stratifications. Our proof of the ampleness criterion is based on a careful analysis of the geometry of these auxiliary varieties.

Let
\mathcal{Z}=(G,P,L,Q,M,\varphi)
be a connected zip datum and
(B,T,z)
be a frame for
\mathcal{Z}
.

Definition 3.14
.

Let
B\subseteq P_{0}\subseteq P
be an intermediate parabolic subgroup. A
G
-zipflag of type
(\mathcal{Z},P_{0})
over a
k
-scheme
S
is a pair
\underline{I}^{P_{0}}=(\underline{I},I_{P_{0}})
, where
\underline{I}=(I,I_{P},I_{Q},\psi)
is a
G
-zip of type
\mathcal{Z}
over
S
, and
I_{P_{0}}\subseteq I_{P}
is a
P_{0}
-torsor.

We define
the stack of
G
-zipflag of type
(\mathcal{Z},P_{0})
, denoted by
G\textnormal{-zipflag}^{\mathcal{Z},P_{0}}
, to be the stack over
k
such that for any
k
-scheme
S
,
G\textnormal{-zipflag}^{\mathcal{Z},P_{0}}(S)
is the groupoid of
G
-zipflags of type
(\mathcal{Z},P_{0})
over
S
. If
P_{0}=P
, this reduces to the original stack of
G
-zips; if
P_{0}=B
, we typically omit the superscript
B
.

Goldring and Koskivirta provide a characterization of this stack in
[
18
,
17
]
.

Theorem 3.15
.

Let

\hat{E}_{P_{0}}:=\{(p,q)\in P\times Q,\ p\in P_{0}\}\subseteq E_{\mathcal{Z}}.

(54)

Let
\hat{E}_{P_{0}}
act on
G
via

(p,q)\cdot g:=p^{-1}gq.

(55)

1. The stack
G\textnormal{-zipflag}^{\mathcal{Z},P_{0}}
is smooth of dimension equal to
\textnormal{dim }P/P_{0}
and is isomorphic to
[\hat{E}_{P_{0}}\backslash G]
.

2. For all parabolic subgroups
B\subseteq P_{1}\subseteq P_{0}\subseteq P
, we have a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\textnormal{-zipflag}^{\mathcal{Z},P_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\textnormal{-zipflag}^{\mathcal{Z},P_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[\hat{E}_{P_{1}}\backslash G]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[\hat{E}_{P_{0}}\backslash G]}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{P_{1},P_{0}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

(56)

3. The projection
\pi_{P_{1},P_{0}}
is proper smooth, with fibers isomorphic to
P_{0}/P_{1}
.

Definition 3.16
.

We define
the partial flag space of type
P_{0}
, denoted by
Y_{P_{0}}
, to be the fiber product

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{P_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\textnormal{-zipflag}^{\mathcal{Z},P_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\textnormal{-zip}^{\mathcal{Z}}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\zeta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{P_{0}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{P_{0}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\zeta}

(57)

Here, we denote both vertical projections
\pi_{P_{0}}
and both horizontal arrows by
\zeta
by abuse of notation. When
P_{0}=B
, we write
Y_{B}=Y
and simply refer to it as the
flag space
.

The coarse stratification.
In
[
18
]
, the authors introduced two stratifications of the stack
G\textnormal{-zipflag}^{\mathcal{Z},P_{0}}
. The strata that will appear in our proof of the ampleness criterion are defined by conditions on the intersections of vector bundles, i.e., conditions of the form
\textnormal{rank }(\mathcal{M}\cap\mathcal{N})\geq t
. This is referred to as the
coarse stratification
by Goldring and Koskivirta.

Put
Q_{0}:=(L\cap P_{0})^{(p)}R_{u}(Q)
. This is a parabolic subgroup of
G
such that

{}^{z}B\subseteq Q_{0}\subseteq Q,\qquad\hat{E}_{P_{0}}\subseteq P_{0}\times Q_{0}.

(58)

Let
I_{0}\subseteq I
and
J_{0}\subseteq J
denote the types of
P_{0}
and
Q_{0}
, respectively. The inclusion of groups induces a natural smooth surjective morphism of stacks

\iota_{P_{0}}:G\textnormal{-zipflag}^{\mathcal{Z},P_{0}}=[\hat{E}_{P_{0}}\backslash G]\longrightarrow[P_{0}\times Q_{0}\backslash G].

(59)

The right hand side is bijective to
{}^{I_{0}}W^{J_{0}}
. We define the
coarse flag strata
of
G\textnormal{-zipflag}^{\mathcal{Z},P_{0}}
as the fibers of this morphism endowed with reduced structure. By further taking the inverse image under
\zeta
, we obtain the
coarse flag strata
of the partial flag space
Y_{P_{0}}
. The closure relation of the coarse strata is given by the Bruhat order. Again, by abuse of language, we refer to the closure of a coarse stratum as a coarse stratum.

Vector bundles on quotient stacks.
Let
G
be a
k
-algebraic group acting on a
k
-scheme
X
. To any finite dimensional representation
(\rho,V)
of
G
, we can associate a vector bundle
\mathcal{V}(\rho)
on the stack
[G\backslash X]
such that

H^{0}([G\backslash X],\mathcal{V}(\rho))=\big\{f:X\longrightarrow V\big|\ f(g\cdot x)=\rho(g)f(x),\quad\forall g\in G,x\in X\big\}.

(60)

Automorphic bundles.
Let
H
be any split connected reductive algebraic group over an arbitrary field
K
with a Borel pair
(B_{H},T_{H})
. For any irreducible representation
(\rho,V)
of
H
, there is a unique highest weight vector
v\in V
with weight
\lambda
. There is a bijection between the set of irreducible representations of
H
over
K
and the set of dominant characters
X^{\ast}_{+}(T_{H})
.

In our case, let
\lambda\in X^{\ast}_{+,I}
be an
I
-regular dominant weight. For any intermediate parabolic subgroup
B\subseteq P_{0}\subseteq P
, let
L_{0}
be the Levi subgroup of
P_{0}
containing
T
and
I_{0}
be the type of
P_{0}
. Then
\lambda
is
I_{0}
-regular dominant. Let
B_{L_{0}}=B\cap L_{0}
, so that
(B_{L_{0}},T)
is a Borel pair of
P_{0}
. The space

V(\lambda):=H^{0}(L_{0}/B_{L_{0}},\mathcal{L}_{\lambda})

(61)

is a representation of
L_{0}
with highest weight
\lambda
. We view this as a representation of
\hat{E}_{P_{0}}
via

\hat{E}_{P_{0}}\xlongrightarrow{\textnormal{pr}_{1}}P_{0}\xlongrightarrow{\pi}L_{0}.

(62)

Here, the first map is the projection onto the first factor and the second is the natural quotient map. By the previous discussion, we obtain a vector bundle, denoted by
\mathcal{V}(\lambda)
, on the stack
G\textnormal{-zipflag}^{\mathcal{Z},P_{0}}=[\hat{E}_{P_{0}}\backslash G]
.

Definition 3.17
.

We call
\mathcal{V}(\lambda)
as well as its pullback
\mathcal{V}_{Y_{P_{0}}}(\lambda)
to
Y_{P_{0}}
by
\zeta

the automorphic vector bundle of weight
\lambda
. If
P_{0}=B
, then
V(\lambda)
is a 1-dimensional representation, we write
\mathcal{V}_{Y}(\lambda)
as
\mathcal{L}_{Y}(\lambda)
instead and refer to it as an
automorphic line bundle of weight
\lambda
.

Definition 3.18
.

Let
\chi:L_{0}\to k^{\times}
be a character of
L_{0}
, again viewed as a character of
\hat{E}_{P_{0}}
. This defines a line bundle
\mathcal{L}(\chi)
on
G\textnormal{-zipflag}^{\mathcal{Z},P_{0}}
and its pullback
\mathcal{L}_{Y_{P_{0}}}(\chi)
on
Y_{P_{0}}
. Note that if
P_{0}=B
, this construction agrees with the one described above.

Remark 3.19
.

For
P_{0}=P
or
B
, the above definition agrees with the classical notion of automorphic vector bundles and automorphic line bundles. Our definition extends this notion to all intermediate partial flag spaces. We believe this extension of definition is natural for the following reasons.

1. They also arise from irreducible representations of parabolic subgroups of
G
, and the pushforward of
V_{Y_{P_{0}}}(\lambda)
to another partial flag space, whenever this is reasonable, corresponds to the parabolic induction. Therefore, the study of such bundles is closely related to representation theory.

2. By the Borel–Weil–Bott theorem, on has

\pi_{\ast}(\mathcal{L}_{Y}(\lambda))=\mathcal{V}_{X}(\lambda).

(63)

By the Kempf vanishing theorem (see
[
26
]
), we moreover have

H^{i}(Y,\mathcal{L}_{Y}(\lambda))=H^{i}(X,\mathcal{V}_{X}(\lambda)).

(64)

Indeed, for any intermediate parabolic subgroups
B\subseteq P_{0}\subseteq P_{1}\subseteq P
, if we denote by
\pi^{P_{0}}_{P_{1}}
the projection
Y_{P_{0}}\to Y_{P_{1}}
, then we have

\pi_{P_{1},\ast}^{P_{0}}(\mathcal{V}_{Y_{P_{0}}}(\lambda))=\mathcal{V}_{Y_{P_{1}}}(\lambda),\quad\textnormal{and}\quad H^{i}(Y_{P_{0}},\mathcal{V}_{Y_{P_{0}}}(\lambda))=H^{i}(Y_{P_{1}},\mathcal{V}_{Y_{P_{1}}}(\lambda)).

(65)

Moreover, these isomorphisms are all equivariant for tame Hecke operators. Hence, the coherent cohomology of
\mathcal{V}_{X}(\lambda)
on
X
, which is of significant interest to experts, is closely related to the coherent cohomology of the automorphic bundles on these partial flag spaces.

3. The proof of the ampleness criterion in this paper heavily relies on these intermediate partial flag spaces and line bundles defined on them. Therefore, it makes sense to unify the notions.

It is natural to pose the following fundamental question.

Question:
For which weights
\lambda
, is the line bundle
\mathcal{L}_{Y}(\lambda)
ample on
Y
?

This paper is devoted to providing a complete answer to this question for unitary Shimura varieties. We believe that the machinery of the geometric description of certain strata (or geometric Jacquet–Langlands correspondence), as well as our method of handling the combinatorical data by induction on "slopes", could be generalized to broader cases. We hope to address this in our future research.

Example 3.20
.

In this example, we provide an explicit construction of the automorphic line bundles used in this paper. We restrict ourselves to the case
G=G\big(\prod_{i=1}^{N}U(m_{i},n_{i})\big)
. Let
X
denote the special fiber of the unitary Shimura variety introduced in Section 2. Recall that the Hodge filtrations on the
\tilde{\tau}_{i}
-side and the
\tilde{\tau}_{i}^{c}
-side are dual to each other, so as long as we have made a refinement of the Hodge filtration on the
\tilde{\tau}_{i}
-component, the refinement on the
\tilde{\tau}_{i}^{c}
-component is immediately given by the dual to it. Hence, we can continue to focus on the
\tilde{\tau}_{i}
-components only. To simplify the notation, we write
\omega_{i}
for
\omega_{\mathcal{A}^{\vee}/X,\tilde{\tau}_{i}}
and
H_{1}^{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{A}/X)_{i}
for
H_{1}^{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{A}/X)_{\tilde{\tau}_{i}}
. By the moduli interpretation,
\omega_{i}
is a vector bundle of rank
m_{i}
. The flag space
Y
parametrizes tuples
(A,\lambda,\eta,\iota,{\textnormal{Fil}^{\bullet}})
, where
(A,\lambda,\eta,\iota)\in X(S)
and
\textnormal{Fil}^{\bullet}
is a refinement of the Hodge filtration that respects the
G
-structure. By this, we mean that for each
1\leq i\leq N
, there is a refinement

\textnormal{Fil}^{i}:0=\mathcal{F}^{i}_{0}\subseteq\mathcal{F}^{i}_{1}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq\mathcal{F}^{i}_{m_{i}-1}\subseteq\mathcal{F}^{i}_{m_{i}}=\omega_{i}\subseteq\mathcal{F}^{i}_{m_{i}+1}\subseteq\cdots\mathcal{F}^{i}_{n-1}\subseteq\mathcal{F}^{i}_{n}=H_{1}^{\mathrm{dR}}(A/X)_{i}.

(66)

Here, the superscript labels the component of the subbundle, and the subscript represents the rank of the corresponding subbundle.

Convention:
In the rest of this paper, we consider the superscripts and subscripts of
\mathcal{F}
as modulo
N
and
n
, respectively. We consider the subscripts of
m
and
n
as modulo
N
. In other words, we identify
\mdmathbb{Z}/N\mdmathbb{Z}
with
\{1,\dots,N\}
. The convention has the ambiguity that
\mathcal{F}^{i}_{n}=H_{1}^{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{A}/X)_{0}
but
\mathcal{F}^{i}_{0}=0
. However, by Lemma
3.21
below, both are zero in the rational Picard group of
X
. Therefore, this ambiguity here is harmless to our purpose.

By definition, in our unitary setup, a weight for
G
corresponds to a tuple of integers

\underline{k}=(k^{i}_{j})^{1\leq i\leq N}_{1\leq j\leq n}.

(67)

The corresponding automorphic line bundle
\mathcal{L}_{Y}(\lambda)
on
Y
is

\mathcal{L}_{Y}(\lambda)=\bigotimes_{i=1}^{N}\bigotimes_{j=1}^{n}(\mathcal{F}^{i}_{j}/\mathcal{F}^{i}_{j-1})^{\otimes k^{i}_{j}}

(68)

The Levi subgroup
L
of
P\subseteq G_{k}
is isomorphic to

\bigg(\prod_{i=1}^{N}(GL_{m_{i}}\times GL_{n_{i}})\bigg)\times\mdmathbb{G}_{m}.

(69)

Therefore, a character of
L
corresponds to a tuple of integers
\chi=(\alpha_{i},\beta_{i})_{i=1}^{N}\in(\mdmathbb{Z}^{2})^{N}
. The corresponding line bundle on
X
is

\mathcal{L}_{X}(\chi)=\bigotimes_{i=1}^{N}\big(\textnormal{det }(H_{1}^{\mathrm{dR}}(A/X)_{i}/\omega_{i})\big)^{\otimes\alpha_{i}}\otimes\big(\textnormal{det }\omega_{i}\big)^{\otimes\beta_{i}}.

(70)

Since we only care about the positivity of
\mathcal{L}_{X}(\chi)
, by Lemma
3.21
below, we can replace
\mathcal{L}_{X}({\chi})
with

\bigotimes_{i=1}^{N}\big(\textnormal{det }\omega_{i}\big)^{\otimes(\beta_{i}-\alpha_{i})}

(71)

in
(\textnormal{Pic $X$})_{\mdmathbb{Q}}
. To simplify the notation, we put
k_{i}=\beta_{i}-\alpha_{i}
and denote

\mathcal{L}_{X}(\chi)=\mathcal{L}_{X}(\{k_{i}\})=\bigotimes_{i=1}^{N}\big(\textnormal{det }\omega_{i}\big)^{\otimes k_{i}}.

(72)

The goal of this paper is therefore to provide a numerical criterion for
[\mathcal{L}_{Y}(\lambda)]
to be ample.

3.5 Statement of the main theorem.

Notation:
Let
\mathcal{M}
be a vector bundle on an algebraic variety
S
. By
[\mathcal{M}]
we mean the class of the line bundle
(\textnormal{det }\mathcal{M})\in(\textnormal{Pic $S$}){Q}
. Our proof of the ampleness (or nefness) criterion is based on computing the intersection numbers, so we will also write
[\mathcal{M}]
for
c_{1}(\mathcal{M})=c_{1}(\textnormal{det }\mathcal{M})
. It follows easily from the definition that
[\mathcal{M}^{(p)}]=p[\mathcal{M}]
when
S
is an
\mdmathbb{F}_{p}
-scheme.

Lemma 3.21
.

Let
(\textnormal{Pic $X$})_{\mdmathbb{Q}}=(\textnormal{Pic $X$})\otimes_{\mdmathbb{Z}}\mdmathbb{Q}
be the rational Picard group of
X
. We have the following relations in
(\textnormal{Pic $X$})_{\mdmathbb{Q}}
.

1.

[\omega_{\mathcal{A}^{\vee}/X,\tilde{\tau}_{i}}]=[\omega_{\mathcal{A}^{\vee}/X,\tilde{\tau}_{i}^{c}}]
.

2.

[H_{1}^{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{A}/X)_{\tilde{\tau}_{i}}]=[H_{1}^{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{A}/X)_{\tilde{\tau}_{i}^{c}}]=0
.

proof:
In the
U(2)
or Hilbert case, the proof can be found in
[
44
]
. Our proof is a natural generalization to the higher rank case.

Recall that for each
i
, the perfect pairing
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
restricts to a perfect pairing

\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{i}:H_{1}^{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{A}/X)_{\tilde{\tau}_{i}}\times H_{1}^{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{A}/X)_{\tilde{\tau}_{i}^{c}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{O}_{X}

(73)

induced by the polarization and the
\mathcal{O}_{E}
-action, such that
\omega_{\mathcal{A}^{\vee}/X,\tilde{\tau}_{i}}
and
\omega_{\mathcal{A}^{\vee}/X,\tilde{\tau}_{i}^{c}}
are annihilators of each other. Thus,
\omega^{\vee}_{\mathcal{A}^{\vee}/X,\tilde{\tau}_{i}}=H_{1}^{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{A}/X)_{\tilde{\tau}_{i}^{c}}/\omega_{\mathcal{A}^{\vee}/X,\tilde{\tau}_{i}^{c}}
and we have

-[\omega_{\mathcal{A}^{\vee}/X,\tilde{\tau}_{i}}]=[H_{1}^{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{A}/X)_{\tilde{\tau}_{i}^{c}}/\omega_{\mathcal{A}^{\vee}/X,\tilde{\tau}_{i}^{c}}].

(74)

Moreover, this pairing satisfies

\langle Vx,y\rangle=\langle x,Fy\rangle^{(p)}

(75)

for
x\in H_{1}^{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{A}/X)_{\tilde{\tau}_{i}}
and
y\in(H_{1}^{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{A}/X)_{\tilde{\tau}_{i+1}^{c}})^{(p)}
. This shows that
(\textnormal{Ker }V_{\tilde{\tau}_{i}})^{\perp}=\textnormal{Ker }V_{\tilde{\tau}_{i}^{c}}
(because
\textnormal{Im }F=\textnormal{Ker }V
). So we have
H_{1}^{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{A}/X)_{\tilde{\tau}_{i}}/\textnormal{Ker }V_{\tilde{\tau}_{i}}=(\textnormal{Ker }V_{\tilde{\tau}_{i}^{c}})^{\vee}
and

[H_{1}^{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{A}/X)_{\tilde{\tau}_{i}}/\textnormal{Ker }V_{\tilde{\tau}_{i}}]=-[\textnormal{Ker $V_{\tilde{\tau}_{i}^{c}}$}].

(76)

We have the short exact sequence

0\longrightarrow\textnormal{Ker $V_{\tilde{\tau}_{i}}$}\longrightarrow H_{1}^{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{A}/X)_{\tilde{\tau}_{i}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V}}{{\longrightarrow}}\omega_{\mathcal{A}^{\vee}/X,\tilde{\tau}_{i+1}}^{(p)}\longrightarrow 0.

(77)

So,

[H_{1}^{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{A}/X)_{\tilde{\tau}_{i}}]=[\textnormal{Ker }V_{\tilde{\tau}_{i}}]+p[\omega_{\mathcal{A}^{\vee}/X,\tilde{\tau}_{i+1}}].

(78)

A similar computation holds for
\textnormal{Ker }V_{\tilde{\tau}_{i}^{c}}
. This shows

p[\omega_{\mathcal{A}^{\vee}/X,\tilde{\tau}_{i+1}}]=p[\omega_{\mathcal{A}^{\vee}/X,\tilde{\tau}_{i+1}^{c}}]-[H_{1}^{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{A}/X)_{\tilde{\tau}_{i}^{c}}].

(79)

Combining the equations (
74
) (for subscript
i+1
) and (
79
), we deduce that

[H_{1}^{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{A}/X)_{\tilde{\tau}_{i}^{c}}]=p[H_{1}^{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{A}/X)_{\tilde{\tau}_{i+1}^{c}}].

(80)

Letting
i
run over
1,\dots,N
, we get

p^{N}[H_{1}^{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{A}/X)_{\tilde{\tau}_{i}^{c}}]=[H_{1}^{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{A}/X)_{\tilde{\tau}_{i}^{c}}].

(81)

Thus,
[H_{1}^{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{A}/X)_{\tilde{\tau}_{i}^{c}}]=0
. Since
H_{1}^{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{A}/X)_{\tilde{\tau}_{i}}
is dual to
H_{1}^{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{A}/X)_{\tilde{\tau}_{i}^{c}}
, the above equality also holds for
H_{1}^{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{A}/X)_{\tilde{\tau}_{i}}
. Besides, (
74
) becomes

[\omega_{\mathcal{A}^{\vee}/X,\tilde{\tau}_{i}}]=[\omega_{\mathcal{A}^{\vee}/X,\tilde{\tau}_{i}^{c}}].

(82)

This completes the proof of the lemma.
\hfill\square

Definition 3.22
.

Let
X
be the special fiber of a unitary Shimura variety of signature
(m_{i},n_{i})
as in Section 2. Let
T=\{i|m_{i}\notin\{0,n\}\}
. We refer to
T
as the
essential set
of
X
, and
t=\#T
as the
essential degree
of
X
.

Definition 3.23
.

For any
i
, let
a(i)
be the smallest positive integer such that
i+1,\dots,\allowbreak i+a(i)-1\notin T
, but
i+a(i)\in T
. Note that we do not assume
i\in T
in this definition.

Since the subscripts of
m
and
n
are viewed as modulo
N
, we should also view the elements in
T
as modulo
N
.

The following are the main theorems of this paper.

Theorem 3.24

(The ampleness criterion for the flag space, case 1)
.

If
t=0
, i.e.,
T
is the empty set, then
\mathcal{L}_{Y}(\lambda)=\mathcal{L}_{Y}(\{k^{i}_{j}\})
is ample if and only if
\lambda
is regular dominant, i.e.,

\displaystyle k^{i}_{n}>k^{i}_{n-1}>\cdots>k^{i}_{1},\quad\forall 1\leq i\leq N.

(83)

Theorem 3.25

(The ampleness criterion for the flag space, case 2)
.

If
t=1
, i.e.,
T=\{i_{1}\}
is a singleton. By definition
a(i_{1})=N
. Assume
m_{i_{1}}=n-1
, then
\mathcal{L}_{Y}(\lambda)=\mathcal{L}_{Y}(\{k^{i}_{j}\})
is ample if and only if

\displaystyle k^{i_{1}}_{n-1}>k^{i_{1}}_{n-2}>\cdots>k_{1}^{i_{1}},

(84)

\displaystyle k^{i}_{n}>k^{i}_{n-1}>\cdots>k^{i}_{1},\qquad\qquad\textnormal{for $i\neq i_{1}$},

\displaystyle\big(\sum_{u=0}^{n-2}p^{Nu}\big)\sum_{j=0}^{N-1}\frac{1}{p^{j}}(k_{1}^{i_{1}+j}-k^{i_{1}+j}_{n})>\sum_{r=2}^{n-1}p^{(r-2)N}\sum_{j=0}^{N-1}\frac{1}{p^{j}}(k^{i_{1}+j}_{r}-k^{i_{1}+j}_{n}).

Theorem 3.26

(The ampleness criterion for the flag space, case 2’)
.

If
t=1
, i.e.,
T=\{i_{1}\}
is a singleton. By definition
a(i_{1})=N
. Assume
m_{i_{1}}=1
, then
\mathcal{L}_{Y}(\lambda)=\mathcal{L}_{Y}(\{k^{i}_{j}\})
is ample if and only if

\displaystyle k^{i_{1}}_{n}>k^{i_{1}}_{n-1}>\cdots>k^{i_{1}}_{2},

(85)

\displaystyle k^{i}_{n}>k^{i}_{n-1}>\cdots>k^{i}_{1},\qquad\qquad\textnormal{for $i\neq i_{1}$},

\displaystyle\big(\sum_{u=0}^{n-2}p^{Nu}\big)\sum_{j=0}^{N-1}\frac{1}{p^{j}}(k_{1}^{i_{1}+j}-k^{i_{1}+j}_{n})>\sum_{r=2}^{n-1}p^{(n-1-r)N}\sum_{j=0}^{N-1}\frac{1}{p^{j}}(k^{i_{1}+j}_{1}-k^{i_{1}+j}_{r}).

Note that this is indeed the dual version to the previous one.

Theorem 3.27

(The ampleness criterion for the flag space, case 3)
.

Suppose that either
t=\#T\geq 2
, or
t=\#T=1
but
m_{i_{1}}\notin\{1,n-1\}
. Let
T=\{i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{t}\}
such that
i_{l}+a(i_{l})=i_{l+1}
for all
l
(Here, we also view
i_{t+1}=i_{1}
). Then the line bundle
\mathcal{L}_{Y}(\lambda)=\mathcal{L}_{Y}(k^{i}_{j})
is ample if and only if

\displaystyle k^{i_{l}}_{m_{i_{l}}}>k^{i_{l}}_{m_{i_{l}}-1}>\cdots>k^{i_{l}}_{1},\quad k^{i_{l}}_{n}>k^{i_{l}}_{n-1}>\cdots>k^{i_{l}}_{m_{i_{l}}+1},\qquad\textnormal{for all $1\leq l\leq t$},

(86)

\displaystyle k^{j}_{n}>k^{j}_{n-1}>\cdots>k^{j}_{1},\qquad\textnormal{for $j\notin T$},

\displaystyle p^{a(i_{l})}\sum_{j=0}^{a(i_{l})-1}\frac{1}{p^{j}}(k^{i_{l}+j}_{1}-k^{i_{l}+j}_{n})>k^{i_{l+1}}_{m_{i_{l+1}}}-k^{i_{l+1}}_{m_{i_{l+1}}+1}\qquad\textnormal{for all $1\leq l\leq t$.}

Theorem 3.28

(The nefness criterion for the flag space)
.

In each of the above cases,
\mathcal{L}_{Y}(\lambda)
is nef if and only if all ">" are replaced by
\geq
.

Variants of the main theorem.
Let
B\subseteq P^{\prime}\subseteq P
be an intermediate parabolic subgroup. Recall that we have introduced the partial flag space
Y_{P^{\prime}}
and the line bundle
\mathcal{L}_{Y_{P^{\prime}}}(\lambda)
associated with a character
\lambda
of the Levi subgroup
L^{\prime}
of
P^{\prime}
.

If
P^{\prime}=P
, then
Y_{P^{\prime}}
coincides with the special fiber
X
. We have the following.

Theorem 3.29

(The ampleness criterion for the Shimura variety)
.

The line bundle

\mathcal{L}_{X}(\{k_{l}\})=\bigotimes_{i=1}^{N}\big(\textnormal{det }\omega_{i}\big)^{\otimes k_{i}}=\bigotimes_{i\in T}\big(\textnormal{det }\omega_{i}\big)^{\otimes k_{i}}

is ample (resp. nef) if and only if
p^{a(i)}k_{i}>k_{i+a(i)}
(resp.
\geq
) for all
i\in T
.

Remark 3.30
.

When
n=2
, the space
X
corresponds to the special fiber of a
U(2)
Shimura variety. The ampleness criterion in this case was previously established by the author in
[
50
]
.

If
P^{\prime}
is a maximal proper parabolic subgroup of
P
, then
Y_{P^{\prime}}
corresponds to refining the Hodge filtration by introducing exactly one new vector bundle
\mathcal{F}^{i}_{j}\subseteq H_{1}^{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{A}/X)_{i}
for some indices
i
and
j
. We require that
\mathcal{F}^{i}_{j}
either contains, or is contained in
\omega_{i}
, depending on the choice of
P^{\prime}
. We denote this parabolic subgroup
P^{\prime}
by
P^{i}_{j}
and the corresponding partial flag space
Y_{P^{i}_{j}}
by
Y^{i}_{j}
. We refer to
Y^{i}_{j}
as a
minimal partial flag space
. More generally, the notation
Y^{i_{1},\dots,i_{r}}_{j_{1},\dots,j_{r}}
refers to the moduli space that records
\{\mathcal{F}^{i_{k}}_{j_{k}}\}
’s (with inclusion conditions given by the parabolic subgroup) for all
1\leq k\leq r
. The type of
P^{i_{1},\dots,i_{r}}_{j_{1},\dots,j_{r}}
is
I^{i_{1},\dots,i_{r}}_{j_{1},\dots,j_{r}}
. These intermediate partial flag spaces will be intensively used in the subsequent proofs.

Definition 3.31
.

Let
P^{\prime}\subseteq P
be an intermediate parabolic subgroup and
Y_{P^{\prime}}
the corresponding partial flag space. If
Y_{P^{\prime}}
records a refinement of the Hodge filtration at
\tau_{i}
, we say that
Y_{P^{\prime}}
is
flagged
at
i
.

Theorem 3.32

(The nefness criterion for partial flag space)
.

Let
P^{\prime}\subseteq P
be an intermediate parabolic subgroup, and
\lambda
be a character of
P^{\prime}
. Then
\mathcal{L}_{Y_{P^{\prime}}}(\lambda)
is nef if and only if its pullback to
Y_{B}=Y
is nef.

Our idea of proving the sufficiency part of the ampleness criterion is to reduce the problem to the nefness criterion for
minimal
partial flag spaces. For convenience, we present the explicit inequalities below. Readers can skip this part first, and come back when reading the proof, especially the induction steps, in Section 7.

Let
P^{i}_{j}
be a maximal proper parabolic subgroup of
P
, and
L^{i}_{j}
a Levi subgroup of
P^{i}_{j}
. A character of the Levi subgroup
L^{i}_{j}
corresponds to a tuple of integers
(\{k_{l}\};\alpha)
. The associated automorphic line bundle on
Y^{i}_{j}
can be written as:

\mathcal{L}_{Y^{i}_{j}}(\{k_{l}\};\alpha)=\mathcal{L}_{Y^{i}_{j}}(k_{1},\dots,k_{N};\alpha)=\big(\textnormal{det }(\omega_{i}/\mathcal{F}^{i}_{j})\big)^{\otimes k_{i}}\otimes(\textnormal{det }\mathcal{F}^{i}_{j})^{\otimes\alpha}\otimes\bigotimes_{\begin{subarray}{c}s=1\\
s\neq i\end{subarray}}^{N}(\textnormal{det $\omega_{s}$})^{\otimes k_{s}}.

(87)

Here and later, if
j>m_{i}
, then
\omega_{i}
is contained in
\mathcal{F}^{i}_{j}
, yet we still write
\omega_{i}/\mathcal{F}^{i}_{j}
to mean
(\mathcal{F}^{i}_{j}/\omega_{i})^{\vee}
.

Corollary 3.33

(The nefness criterion for minimal partial space)
.

We write
T=\{i_{1},\dots,i_{t}\}
. Assumptions as above, we assume further that
P^{\prime}=P^{i}_{j}
is a maximal proper parabolic subgroup.

1.

If
T
is the empty set, then
\mathcal{L}_{Y^{i}_{j}}(\{k_{l}\};\alpha)
is nef if and only if

k_{i}\geq\alpha.

(88)

From now on we assume that
T
is non-empty, then there is a unique
1\leq l_{0}\leq t
, such that
i\in\{i_{l_{0}},i_{l_{0}}+1,\dots,i_{l_{0}+1}-1\}
. For simplicity, we assume that
i_{1}\leq i
.

2.

If
T
is a singleton, namely,
T=\{i_{1}\}
, then
l_{0}=1
by definition. If moreover
m_{i_{1}}=n-1
, then
\mathcal{L}_{Y^{i}_{j}}(\{k_{l}\};\alpha)
is nef if and only if

\displaystyle k_{i}\geq\alpha,

(89)

\displaystyle(p^{(n-j)N}-1)\big(k_{i_{1}}-\frac{1}{p^{i-i_{1}}}(k_{i}-\alpha)\big)\geq(p^{(n-j-1)N}-1)k_{i_{1}}.

3.

If
T
is a singleton, namely,
T=\{i_{1}\}
, then
l_{0}=0
by definition. If moreover
m_{i_{1}}=1
, then
\mathcal{L}_{Y^{i}_{j}}(\{k_{l}\};\alpha)
is nef if and only if

\displaystyle k_{i}\geq\alpha,

(90)

\displaystyle(p^{jN}-1)\big(k_{i_{1}}-\frac{1}{p^{i-i_{1}}}(k_{i}-\alpha)\big)\geq(p^{(j-1)N}-1)k_{i_{1}}.

4.

In the rest cases, i.e., either
t\geq 2
, or
t=1
but
m_{i_{1}}\notin\{1,n-1\}
. Then
\mathcal{L}_{Y^{i}_{j}}(\{k_{l}\};\alpha)
is nef if and only if

\displaystyle k_{i}\geq\alpha,

(91)

\displaystyle p^{a(i_{j})}k_{i_{j}}\geq k_{i_{j+1}},\qquad\textnormal{for $j\neq l_{0}$},

\displaystyle p^{a(i_{l_{0}})}\big(k_{i_{l_{0}}}-\frac{1}{p^{i-i_{l_{0}}}}(k_{i}-\alpha)\big)\geq k_{i_{l_{0}}+1}.

The author encounters a difficulty in proving the necessity part of the ampleness criterion on partial flag spaces, since we cannot pull ample line bundles back via proper morphisms. Nevertheless, the nefness criterion for partial flag spaces suffices for our purpose of understanding the ampleness criterion of
\mathcal{L}_{Y}(\lambda)
.

4  Calculus on the special fiber

The primary approach to the ampleness criterion involves doing intersection theory on the special fiber and the associated (partial) flag spaces. However, when dealing with certain low-dimensional strata, it turns out that neither the theory of
G
-zips nor the theory of Schubert calculus on flag varieties provide sufficiently many divisor classes. A crucial step in addressing this problem is to establish correspondences between the strata of different Shimura varieties. This enables us to relate automorphic line bundles for different unitary Shimura varieties and make suitable inductive arguments.

This idea was initially introduced in Helm’s work
[
24
]
for sparse strata on
U(2)
Shimura varieties. It was subsequently extended by
[
44
]
to
U(2)
and quaternionic Shimura varieties, and further generalized by
[
23
,
48
]
to a broader framework. In this chapter, we present a simplified version tailored to our specific needs.

4.1 Review of Dieudonné theory

In this section, let
k
be a perfect field of characteristic
p
, and let
W(k)
denote its ring of Witt vectors. The Frobenius endomorphism
\sigma
on
W(k)
lifts the Frobenius map on the residue field
k
